For, dear reader! what does that mean, and what a strange and incomprehensible thing is it, that children in an entire country revolt, whose disobedience (if it is otherwise a disobedience, when one obeys God more than men) consists in this: that they want to **pray by force**, and want to pray publicly before the eyes of all the world, and want to pray more than one can demand of them or can allow them; whereas otherwise all children must be driven to prayer with much effort. This matter is so difficult and paradoxical that no man, with all his arts, could have brought about such an **uprising for prayer** <note>The author uses "Aufstand" (uprising/rebellion) ironically to describe the children's refusal to stop praying</note>, even if it had been commanded of him. Such an uncommon love for prayer that children give up sleep and food for it, and some even remain fasting until the evening in order to be more fit for prayer; such great patience in frost and cold, and in the most burdensome weather, always unchanging constancy; the good modesty and order maintained by most in their prayer hours; the obedience willingly rendered to their quite strictly ruling comrades; the singular devotion witnessed by many, the likes of which one is rarely accustomed to finding even among old people; the requested account-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Children’s Prayer movement in Silesia (circa 1707), focusing on the theological interpretation of why God has moved the hearts of children to pray in the fields while their parents and the social elite remain spiritually stagnant.</meta>

hand of the Lord / which is so wonderful / and so strange now over us in these lands. Let everyone humble himself before this great God / and say with Paul: How unsearchable are His judgments / and His ways past finding out! <note>A reference to Romans 11:33.</note> But let us also perform heartfelt repentance / and ask Him / that He would turn everything / that happens in our days / to our best. He allows us in all places so many hundreds / yes, thousands of little pray-ers / under the open sky / to kneel down upon the earth: Perhaps the parents of these children have prayed very little before this / and now these children must be their judges. Therefore let the old learn from the young / to be devout / and to pray all the more diligently: For it is high time in our <term>Fatherland</term> <note>original: "Vaterlande"; here referring to the region of Silesia within the Holy Roman Empire.</note> to pray / and if we do not make God our heavenly Father our friend through prayer and supplication / then such days might well come in the future / when parents and children will have to lament and weep together.

§. 24. Because the miserable are disturbed / and the poor sigh / I will arise / says the Lord / I will provide a help / that one shall teach with confidence. §. 25. 1. That this movement arose among the children within 5 days throughout a region of five principalities / whereas it would have been humanly impossible / for the children to have given each other guidance in this matter.
2. That even children of 5 to 6 years / or at least of such an age / where otherwise nothing but inconsistency is found / are moved in such a way / which one would otherwise hardly find in one child or another / let alone in such a great multitude.
3. That there is such a great harmony throughout the children / which would otherwise be very difficult to find in a single town or village / let alone in several principalities.
4. That they use the same method in the main / without having consulted one another about it.
5. That between the hymns they sing in their assemblies / and that which they read, the most beautiful harmony and connection is found / which is otherwise beyond the capacity <note>original: "Captum"</note> of such children.
6. That, contrary to all the usual flightiness of youth / they hold together in constant devotion / despite the fact that they could be disturbed in various ways / especially by the crowds of people flocking to see them.

7. That the roughest people / for example / peasants / and officers / who attend their **devotions** <note>In this context, a "devotion" refers to an informal prayer meeting or gathering.</note> / are powerfully moved by it / and stirred to tears.
8. That their fervor is so great / that it can be recognized not only by the entire conduct of their prayer / but also by the fact that they pay no heed / even to kneeling in places / where it is entirely filthy.
9. That many adults have also improved themselves in such a way / that they no longer visit the **taverns** <note>original: *Sauff-Häuser*, literally "drinking-houses."</note> / and since that time / many other good movements and improvements have been observed.
10. That they do not allow themselves to be deterred from this devotion by opposition.
11. That they do not stop their assemblies even in the worst weather / but one and all still arrive for them.
12. That / although some were locked up / they nevertheless sought opportunities / to come to the other children / and for that reason / as stated in some reports / jumped out of windows / or broke through the stove / or / as stated in another letter / sought to do the same.
13. That / when they were no longer allowed to go out through the gates in **Ligniz** <note>Ligniz, known today as Legnica in Poland, was a major city in the region of Silesia.</note> / they gathered in the public market with a **boldness** <note>original: *parrhesie*, a Greek term meaning "fearless or candid speech."</note> quite unusual for children / and performed their prayers there.

public market / and performed their prayers there.
14. That in their prayer they are not against / but in favor of public worship / in that they implore God for the **restoration** of their churches / and otherwise call upon God in their prayer for nothing other / than what is according to His Word.
15. That they do not pray **by the mere performance of the act** <note>original: *ex opere operato*, a Latin theological term referring to a ritual performed as a mechanical duty rather than from the heart.</note> / but ask God for a true improvement of life / and encourage one another toward the same.
16. That they persevere in prayer two or three times a day and indeed quite early / when it was still dark / also according to some reports / for three / yes even / according to other reports / and in some places nearly 5 hours.
17. That they maintain a very fine order / when they go home again / in that they modestly take one another by the hand / and walk away in pairs.
18. That they also behave themselves quite quietly and modestly at home / and wherever else they are.
19. That the **constable** <note>original: *Häschker*, a term for a low-ranking law enforcement officer or bailiff.</note> / who was sent to whip them away from a place / but found them praying on their faces / went back with tears / and apologized to his superiors that he had done nothing to them / because they had done nothing evil / but had only prayed.

21. That other sensible and experienced people / who at first considered such movements to be merely **childishness** / are now convinced by the testimony of the children / that this stems from a higher impulse.

In noting these **signs** <note>original: *Kennzeichen*; the author uses this term to mean evidentiary marks of divine origin.</note> / that the matter is good / and precious / it must be remembered / that such signs are taken not from visual evidence / but as indicated / from written reports. Should any circumstances in the reports received not be in accordance with the truth / then those signs that are based on such inconsistent reports would fall away. Furthermore, it is not the opinion / that where such a sign is found / one must immediately consider the whole matter to be good / and divine / rather this is the conclusion: where all signs / or at least the most prominent ones are found / then one has no reason / to consider it as anything other / than something good / and divine / at least as long as nothing is found to the contrary / from which it could be sufficiently proven / that it is ungodly. Those / who lead the children into such excesses / have the greater sin. If clergy are also found / who disapprove of the matter / it does not follow for that reason / that the matter is ungodly / but rather it must be investigated / whether they have sufficient grounds / to **disapprove** <note>original: *improbiren*.</note> of the matter. the Pharisees disapproved <note>original: *improbirtens*; to reject or condemn as improper.</note> / and nonetheless the matter was so divine / that according to the testimony of our Savior, the Scriptures were fulfilled in that very thing. Finally / if the children do not do it well or divinely enough / and one finds much to criticize in their prayers / then the elders should see to it / that they do better / and set such an example before their eyes in all matters / so that they may take it as a pattern of how one should lead one's Christianity not coldly or lukewarmly / but with a true divine zeal / and fervor. For since the children are now on such a good path / they will follow all the more gladly / if they might now also have good leaders among the elders.

once happened with Moses in Numbers 11:26 and the following verses. When this great zealot for the ceremonies of divine service—which he had not taken from the ancients, but had learned from God Himself—heard a complaint against two men, Eldad and Medad <note>In the Bible, these two men began prophesying in the camp rather than at the Tabernacle, causing Joshua to ask Moses to stop them.</note>, who began to prophesy in the camp in an extraordinary way that was nevertheless pleasing to God; and when Joshua himself pleaded that they should be forbidden, Moses gave this emphatic answer: **“Are you jealous for my sake? Would to God that all the Lord’s people prophesied, and that the Lord would put His spirit upon them!”** The narrowness of the space prevents us from proceeding further in the application of this story; perhaps, however, another time will provide a greater opportunity for it. These are, to me, two healthy and rational judgments that the author has shared with posterity from reports previously obtained. I have also received another judgment regarding the children from one of my friends, which sounds quite favorable and reads as follows: I must confess beforehand that the Silesian Children’s Prayer <note>A spontaneous religious revival among children in Silesia in 1707, where they gathered in fields to pray for peace.</note> appeals to me as something good, specifically because it receives so much opposition, just as Christ was a sign that was spoken against. It is also, in my opinion, a good thing that the children—wherever they may have been given the occasion by the Swedes <note>Referring to the Swedish army of Charles XII, whose soldiers were known for their public prayers during the Great Northern War.</note>—vied with the rough soldiers 

<column-break/>

<page-num>111</page-num>

<meta>catchword: themselves</meta>

and so steadfastly adopted what is good, in contrast to the sluggish spirit of others. No one who has seen it will deny that this is a work of God, for it is evident from all circumstances that they begin it with a childlike trust in God’s goodness. As for the fact that others have imitated the first ones, I see the finger of God in that as well. For one often has examples of a pious child in a house who takes delight in prayer and other good exercises, yet the others do not follow him for that reason, but rather likely mock him for it. Therefore, what they have imitated from one another in simplicity and with faithful, childlike hearts did not happen out of a quest for glory <term>vainglory: the desire for social status or recognition</term>—which is very much in vogue among the so-called clergy—but rather out of a heartfelt zeal, so that they would not be the last in prayer. Furthermore, the children neglect neither churches nor schools, but have arranged their prayer hours so that they are performed either before or after the sermon. And granted, should it have happened once or twice <note>referring to missing a formal service</note>, the great question is whether they could not have been more edified through the devout reading of a chapter from the Bible, and from hymns and prayers, than from other over-elaborate sermons <note>The author critizes the "artificial" or "high-style" preaching common in Lutheran churches of the time, which children found difficult to understand.</note> which they would not have understood? Indeed, it has been truly observed that children have improved themselves and others thereby, which so many sermons were not able to accomplish in them. Many came to the praying children out of curiosity but left quite changed, having felt a pull toward goodness in their hearts. If these children had not been disturbed, many more from the large cities might have been converted by them; and the children could have been the forerunners for the teachers and preachers, so that afterward their sermons—when delivered in the power of the Spirit—would have been more feared <note>In this context, "feared" implies being held in deep, transformative respect or awe.</note>. Even if there were some who gave gifts to the praying children—who are poor children regardless—they have been no more provoked to greed or to making a business out of godliness than a child becomes greedy when their parents give them a reward for their diligence. Alas! Would that many preachers were not so set on serving only for wages—which is noticeable when the authorities assign them a catechization <note>A formal session of religious instruction through questions and answers.</note> for which they receive no extra compensation. Children have even been found in the suburbs of Breslau <note>Modern-day Wrocław, Poland; at the time, a major center of the Silesian region.</note> who refused to take money, explicitly stating they did so so their slanderers could not say they prayed for the sake of money. But if true shepherds were there—as I well know some still exist—and if they went into the midst of the little flock and guided them with both teaching and example, what a blessing we would have! What a fine congregation we would obtain in the future from this good opportunity! But now most have no ears for this, instead asking the children how much longer they intend to pray. They present them with all sorts of pointed questions, asking under which part of the Catechism this or that passage of prayer or song belongs; and if they cannot answer immediately, they are spoken to harshly and frightened. It is quite absurd that people expect perfection from children while excusing the great ignorance of the elderly. May God have mercy, and in the meantime, may the babbling of children please Him for Christ's sake, Amen!

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Silesian Children’s Prayer" movement (Kinderbeten) of 1707–1708. While the previous page defended the children’s sincerity against critics, this page begins a formal report on the movement's origins in the Duchy of Glogau and its organized structure.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>-(0)-</header>

Germany <note>Likely a reference to a specific contemporary chronicle or news periodical titled *Teutschland*.</note> discusses on page 115 the **Silesian Children** according to the following report. During the ongoing religious proceedings (while the high Ministers of the Imperial and Swedish crowns were negotiating with one another regarding the churches to be restored to the adherents of the Augsburg Confession <note>The primary confession of faith for the Lutheran Church; the negotiations mentioned relate to the Treaty of Altranstädt (1707), which restored religious rights to Silesian Lutherans.</note> in Silesia), a matter so strange occurred in Silesia that one can, in my opinion, say the like has never been heard of, not only for as long as Christianity has flourished, but for as long as the world has stood. Either at the very beginning of this year, or a short time before (at Christmas), children of both male and female sexes—among whom the oldest had reached approximately 13 to 14 years of age—came upon thoughts otherwise unusual for their age: to assemble daily in a certain place, two or three times, for prayer. This childlike devotion, as far as I have been able to obtain information here, first began in **Sprottau** <note>Modern-day Szprotawa, Poland.</note>, a city located in the **Principality of Glogau** <note>Modern-day Głogów, Poland.</note>, and subsequently spread through various other principalities in Silesia, where it remains in full swing even up to the present hour. The origin may well be attributed to the Swedish troops who were stationed for a time in the said Principality last autumn, and their well-known "prayer hours" <note>Public prayer sessions held by the Swedish military, which were highly disciplined and visible to the local population.</note>. The order of their practice, however, is as follows, though it does not match in every detail across

<column-break/>

<page-num>115</page-num>
<header>-(0)-</header>
<sig>H 2</sig>

each and every assembly of children. In the morning at the break of day, these praying children come together from the towns and villages in the open field and form either a single circle consisting of boys and girls—yet in such a way that the girls all stand close together without being mixed among the boys—or the boys form a separate circle, as do the girls. This gathering has chosen a precentor <note>A leader of congregational singing.</note>, or as it were, a **spiritual commander**, who is generally the oldest, most capable, and most sensible among them. When he has formally arranged the circle and placed his young congregation in order, he goes into the center, lies down with his face to the ground along with all the others, and they pray the **Lord's Prayer** <note>The "Our Father."</note> with one mind in a low voice. Then they stand up again to sing several spiritual hymns, among which the most common are: ***Dearest Jesus, we are here.*** <note>Original: "Liebster JEsu / wir sind hier," a famous Lutheran hymn by Tobias Clausnitzer.</note> ***The time is surely drawing near.*** <note>Original: "Es ist gewißlich an der Zeit," a hymn concerning the Last Judgment.</note> ***You Prince of Peace, Lord Jesus Christ,*** <note>Original: "Du Friedens-Fürst HErr JEsu Christ," a hymn for peace.</note> and so forth. After the singing is completed, they fall to their knees, and the precentor prays several psalms, prayers, and spiritual verses very well-suited to the present time, and concludes with these words: **"The Lord bless us and keep us,"** after which the hymn **"Now praise God! It is finished"** <note>Original: "nun GOtt Lob! es ist vollbracht."</note> is sung at the very last, and the commander commands them...

...commanding <note>The text continues from the previous page describing the "leader" of the children's prayer groups, often a slightly older boy.</note> them at a certain hour / for example in the morning at 11:00 and in the afternoon at 4:00 at this place / to appear in proper discipline and decency / which he knows how to present by virtue of a seriousness mixed with love. Since no governance <note>original: *Regiment*</note> can long endure without the punishment of transgressors / he makes use of a stick / or rod / and strikes with all earnestness those who do not behave properly / whereat they bear such thing with the greatest patience / no differently than if they had sworn an oath to the colors <note>To "swear to the flag/colors" refers to military enlistment; the author is marveling that children show the same discipline as professional soldiers.</note>. One must marvel / that these children in all places / during their devotion / display a singular modesty / in that they do not even move their eyes while singing and praying / much less allow themselves to be distracted / even though a great multitude of people of both high and low status watches them. Their zeal / to find themselves in these unusual assemblies / is so intense / that they often cannot sleep for an entire night out of longing / but rather wait for the following day with burning desire. From this they allow neither the authorities <term>Obrigkeit: the civil or local government officials</term> / nor the prohibitions of their parents to hold them back / unless one restrains them by force / and it has been experienced in some places / that they went without fear to the lords / and urgently pleaded / not to be hindered in their free exercise of prayer. Among other things, it occurred in the mountains <note>Likely the Riesengebirge (Giant Mountains) on the border of Silesia and Bohemia.</note> / that a farmer his boy /

<column-break/>

<page-num>117</page-num>
<header>*(0)*</header>
<sig>H 3</sig>

boy / and girl locked in a chamber / so that they should not run out before the village with others of their kind / to pray / and to sing. In this arrest the boy said to the girl / that if their father would not let them out / they would jump out of the window. When the girl gave in answer / that she would be in danger of breaking her neck / the former replied / that **GOD** would surely preserve them / and carry them upon his hands. Because the father / who had overheard everything from outside / stood in worry / that his children might perhaps venture a dangerous leap out of desire for a martyr's crown / he opened the door / and let them run unhindered to their praying comrades in the field. In other places it has been noticed / that / if one did not let the children go to the assembly according to their will / they became sick with longing / or fell into a faint. I will meanwhile / regarding this wondrous children’s worship <term>Kinder-Gottesdienst: literally "children's God-service"; the term used for these spontaneous prayer meetings</term> / append the following two considerations / although the author of the same is unknown to me.

### Thorough Considerations in the Fear of GOD / whether the children of the Evangelicals <note>In the 18th century, "Evangelical" was the standard term for Lutherans.</note> should be forbidden from prayerful devotion!

1. Whether Christ is not the only ground / goal / and purpose of our salvation <term>Seeligkeit: eternal happiness or the state of being saved</term>?
2. Whether knowing / and praising Christ / is not the highest wisdom?

3. Whether one can reach this goal—as the worldly <note>original: *Fleischlichen*, literally "the fleshly"</note> have tried until now—without prayer, singing, and Bible reading?
4. Whether public Sunday and holiday exercises, which occur in full distraction, are sufficient for this?
5. Whether the memorization of the Catechism, forced by beatings, makes a Christian?
6. Whether this—if it is not instilled with emphasis and explained through heart-moving interpretation alongside fervent prayer—makes anything other than hypocrites?
7. Whether the effect of this is not as clear as day within the Evangelical <note>In this period, "Evangelical" refers specifically to the Lutheran Church.</note> churches?
8. Whether children have not been pointed toward Christ by parents and teachers until now?
9. Whether children—given that they have dedicated to Christ their idle time, which is otherwise used for evil, but are now frightened away from it—must not inevitably become atheists?
10. Whether this, when it is done by Evangelicals, is not the most cruel tyranny and persecution, the likes of which one has never read in history?
11. Whether the pagans, in their persecutions, were not far more excusable, because they forbade the worship of a God in whom they themselves did not believe?

<meta>catchword: able</meta>

<page-num>119</page-num>

able for the children, because they are kept away from Christ, to whom they were previously directed?

14. Whether one must not, in this case, obey GOD more than men?
15. Whether GOD does not desire to have praise prepared for Himself out of the mouths of young children and infants? <note>A reference to Psalm 8:2 or Matthew 21:16.</note>
16. Whether an Evangelical man is to be found who has the power to restrict prayer, singing, and Bible reading in churches, schools, and houses?
17. From whence has he received such authority?
18. Whether an Evangelical has the freedom to suppress the Spirit in others by force, and whether this is not the worst kind of tyranny?
19. Whether, apart from a customary and accepted external <term>work performed <note>original: *Opere operato*—the theological idea that a religious ritual is effective simply by the act of doing it, regardless of the person's heart.</note></term>, no Evangelical may otherwise honor or serve GOD?
20. Whether the late Blessed Luther did not himself consider clinging to the external to be a "phantom of the Devil"? Volume 2 of the Jena German Edition, folio 103a.
21. Whether it is therefore not certain that the external is worth nothing without the internal!
22. Whether one can, however, force the internal—especially in children—at the specific time that one wishes?

->§. 28. The very same author who composed the previous questions has added the following 43 questions.<-

1. Whether the youth of the Principalities of Liegnitz, Jauer, Glogau, and part of Schweidnitz and Wohlau <note>These were territories in Silesia (modern-day Poland) where the "Children’s Prayer" movement was most active around 1707–1708.</note> were not inflamed in love and fiery devotion to God within a period of five days?
2. Whether anyone would have been fast enough or capable enough in such a short time to make this known in all places, in all houses, indeed among all children?

<summary>These questions conclude a critique of the "cold" and "forced" nature of the established Lutheran Church, contrasting it with the spontaneous, fiery devotion of the Silesian children. The text argues that the sudden, widespread nature of the children's movement across several principalities is evidence of divine intervention rather than human planning.</summary>

<keywords>Silesian Children's Prayer, Lutheran Orthodoxy, Pietism, means of grace, religious persecution, Liegnitz, Jauer, Glogau, Schweidnitz, Wohlau, devotional practice</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the numbered defense of the Silesian children's prayer movement. The author argues that the children's sudden transformation from "unruly" to "devout" is evidence of divine intervention rather than human orchestration or mere "works of the flesh."</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>
<header>122  (o)</header>

3. Whether similar universal decrees <note>original: *Universalien*; in this context, it refers to general royal proclamations or circular orders.</note> for a general summons to serve God could have been issued by the greatest monarch on earth with enough power and penetration to actually bring about such an effect?
4. If this were a natural occurrence, where did the resulting obedience, readiness, and order come from?
5. Who was the "Master of Ceremonies" who instructed those who—until now—had practiced their devotions in a disorderly way and without any piety? Who led these children—who had otherwise become wild and unruly—in a single moment into such a quiet state, as demonstrated by their behavior when entering and leaving? For shortly before this, they had behaved petulantly and wickedly in school and church.
6. Who taught them to find such applicable hymns for all the Psalms and chapters, to the shame of the elders, and even some of the learned themselves?
7. Who made the arrangements for their devotions and publicized them to the children?
8. Who placed them so quickly into such reverence toward God that they would not be moved to look around even once, even if fifteen to twenty horses were ridden toward them multiple times? <note>This likely refers to accounts of soldiers or spectators attempting to disrupt the children's prayer circles by riding horses toward them, only to find the children remained motionless in prayer.</note>
9. Is this deep devotion likely to be sought, sensed, or found among both the old and the young in the churches?
10. Whether this is not, therefore, something much greater and higher than what is commonly found among the Evangelicals <note>At this time, "Evangelical" (*Evangelisch*) was the standard term for the Lutheran Church.</note>?

<column-break/>

<page-num>123</page-num>
<header>(o)  123</header>

11. Whether it is not a pure "work of the flesh" <note>A biblical term referring to human impulses or pride rather than spiritual inspiration.</note>, or perhaps an over-eager, worldly caution, when an Evangelical seeks to destroy what God is doing?
12. Whether the opposition of an Evangelical does not stem from a desire to hide his own ignorance, blindness, and foolishness—indeed, his own previous hypocrisy and lukewarm service to God—so they are not exposed and discovered?
13. Whether it is a natural thing, or a work of the flesh, to sing and pray with true, fiery devotion, without any distraction or movement?
14. Does the earth, and everything within it, not belong to the Lord?
15. Is it not, therefore, permitted and right to laud and praise the Lord in all places?
16. Did Christ our Savior <note>original: *Heyland*</note> ever restrict His followers to certain specific places?
17. Did the Holy Spirit prescribe to them only certain hours on Sunday?
18. Did the first Christians come together only on Sundays or holidays to pray, praise God, and edify one another?
19. Did they not meet in all places, rather than always in just one single location?
20. Did the first Christians not meet many times a day to pray and praise God?
21. Is it not a work of the flesh, and a clear proof of falling away from faith, that this no longer happens today?
22. Whether the dear children are to be blamed, since they...

...should they, despite the resistance of all carnal men, praise God often in the manner of the first Christians?
23. Whether this is something new, since over 1000 years ago it was the only way for true Christians?
24. Whether the elders should not rather be led to repentance by this, and be convinced of their own carnal state?
25. Or whether it is not a work of the flesh if a Protestant is provoked to bitterness or persecution against the innocent because of it?
26. Whether this—if it is done by a Protestant—is not the most cruel tyranny and persecution?
27. Whether it is not clear that such a person is the Anti-Christ, or the "Counter-Christ"?
28. Whether it does not convince all other religions that the Protestants, under the cloak of the Christian name, strive only for a pagan existence apart from the <term>opere operato</term>? <note>original: *opere operato*; a Latin theological phrase meaning "by the work performed." The author is suggesting that without genuine spirit, Protestant rituals are as empty as the "mechanical" rituals they often criticized in Catholicism.</note>
29. Whether it is more pleasing to God for children to spend three hours of the day running around, cursing, telling vulgar jokes, and fighting, etc., as has happened everywhere until now, or to spend them in prayer?
30. Whether a rational man can be found who can deny that this has been the ordinary pastime of rowdy youth among those who call themselves Protestant Christians until now?
31. Whether parents, pastors, and schoolmasters have kept them <meta>from</meta>

from it with true labor, diligence, and sufficient oversight?

33. Whether the <term>pretext</term> <note>original: *Prätext*; an excuse or "cover story."</note> will excuse the Protestants before Christ’s judgment seat if they claim they feared heresy might be hidden within these prayers?
34. Whether Protestant teachers, especially in the villages, do not have enough time to pay attention to this?
35. Or has Christ not earned this little bit of effort for their sake?
36. Or are the poor children, in their current extreme blindness, not in need and worthy of it?
37. fear and devotion / and whether such uncommon, modest discipline among otherwise rowdy children should not have convinced all other religions of the goodness of our fellow believers?

42. Or whether this can truly happen through the meager <term>mechanical ritual</term> <note>original: *opus operatum*; the idea that a religious act is effective regardless of the heart of the person performing it.</note> and the otherwise mostly pagan lifestyle?

43. Or whether it is not beyond human reason / that although these tender children are kept from such an open divine service from their youth / which in most places has been presented to them as suspicious / they nevertheless do not let themselves be deterred in many places by the threats and punishments of their parents and teachers / but remain faithful to God / and continue in a devotion that must move even a heart of stone?

§. 29. The person who drafted these and the previous questions / and submitted them to the hostile-minded to be answered / is a person of high rank / who eagerly seeks God in Christ / and leads their entire court toward godliness / who is also well-acquainted with all the circumstances of the praying children in Silesia / whom I also know very well / and through whose company I have found myself not a little strengthened. But the other person / who publishes the *European Fame* <note>original: *Europäische Famam*; a popular contemporary journal that reported on current events across Europe.</note> / has noted another particular observation / that those who disapprove of the assembly of the children / and wish that they might have been abolished long ago / cannot easily accuse these tender

children of heresy / as the children know nothing of it / which they certainly would have done / and would have accused them now of this, now of that heretical error / through their usual conclusions / if they had found adult men before them / which they cannot do now. Since they nevertheless set upon the children / they must say something or other / and so they pretend / and are concerned / that the praying children might harbor a secret snake in their bosom / which would spew its poison in due time / but this would only be the time to raise an objection if such a poison had actually manifested itself. Otherwise / wherever something truly good has sprouted / one could immediately worry that something evil might gradually mix in with it / and in such a manner / and according to such vain objections / no Gospel could be preached / nor any good seed sown / because the enemy might come / and sow weeds among the wheat. In such matters, one must above all else see / whether the work in and of itself is good / and founded in God’s Word / if one finds it to be so / then one must approve and praise it / and have hope in God / that He will preserve the good wheat / and the children of the Kingdom from error. One thing is certain / that the children in Silesia have gained much benefit through their constant, diligent prayer / and even drew a Jewish girl to them by their devotion / and did not push her away / when they heard that she believes

that Christ also died for them / and for that reason had publicly confessed / that they now experienced / that God had opened a door for Jews and Gentiles / and wanted to convert them / and have them be saved. It has also been observed / that those who were the song-leaders among the children had previously been the most godless / and unruly boys of all their comrades / but have now become obedient to their parents / or even / where they did not have the best parents / they nevertheless drew them toward God with all humility and through their good example / and edified them more without words / through their quiet nature. One must also confess / that these children now / as the *European Fame* <note>original: *Europäische Fama*; a contemporary journal</note> page 132 testifies / go to school far more willingly / and without being forced / than they were previously accustomed to / and that during the <term>Lenten season</term> they exerted themselves most zealously / to serve God with prayer / and singing / while the old people were intent / on sacrificing their souls to the devil with gluttony and drinking / and other excesses / so that one can say without absurdity / that at this time the old people have been transformed into wanton children / and conversely the children into godly old people. Another judgment / written in Silesia / and composed by a preacher there / was communicated in Berlin / where it was sent to a preacher at St. Nicholas Church / which reads as follows: May the Lord have mercy on us / and grant / that we prepare ourselves in patience / and bestow in all suffering the strength to triumph in Christ! The

---

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>~(0)~</header>

Church <note>original: *Kirche*</note> will likely have to enter the furnace of misery / so that the impure dross / with which it is completely covered / may be burned out! Afterward God Himself will surely build them a holy Zion / for which He will require neither worldly power / nor political <term>councils</term> <note>original: *Concilia*</note>. And perhaps a prelude to this is the great movement / that has been perceived here for some time among the poor youth / about which you will have been informed from the news. To write briefly of it / the matter is as follows. The boys / and girls from ages 4 to 12 / and even 14 years / assemble out of their own impulse / partly on the mountains / partly in the fields and meadows / partly in the churchyards / and then also in the church / and hold their prayer hours mostly while kneeling in the following manner. 1. They read something from the Bible / either a Psalm / or another chapter. 2. They sing a hymn of repentance. 3. They all fall together upon their knees / and pray a general prayer / for example <note>original: *e. gr.* / *exempli gratia*</note> the Litany / or the hymn / *Faithful Watchman of Israel* / or / *O Great God of Power!* After this they pray some Psalms again / fall again upon their knees / and pray. After the singing and prayer are completed / they speak the benediction / and depart from one another. During the day such a prayer hour is held three times / and the devotion therein is irreproachable. I have not been able to awaken such a spirit of worship in the <term>catechism classes</term> <note>original: *Catechisationibus*</note> / as I have perceived among them in such prayer hours. I write here of the matter /

as I have seen here in our <term>congregation</term> <note>original: Gemeine</note> with my own eyes / and indeed not without tears / although the <term>fervor</term> <note>original Latin: fervor; meaning intense religious heat or passion</note> among the children is now somewhat subsiding. How it may be in other places in this regard / I have not inquired. It is certain / that there will be few cities and villages / where one would not have seen such devotion / among the children. Various <term>judgments</term> <note>original Latin: Judicia</note> are being passed upon this. Some consider it a divine impulse / others, however, think the children learned it from the Swedes <note>referring to the Swedish army under Charles XII, whose presence in Silesia in 1707 pressured the Emperor to grant religious liberties to Protestants</note> / others even fear / that <term>Quakerism</term> <note>a radical Christian movement often accused by the orthodox of valuing personal "inner light" over scripture</note> might be lurking behind it / and lead to personal revelations / which is why they forbid it to the children. We, in our place, have let the matter take its free course / and have seen to it / that no disorders / which are common among youth / might creep in. I am far too humble to judge this / yet to reveal my few thoughts / which I have on the matter / it almost seems to me / as on <term>Palm Sunday</term> / when those in the spiritual and worldly estates who should have gone out to meet the Savior / and welcome Him / sat silent / and the children were then awakened / to raise their <term>Hosanna</term>. So it goes among us as well. When Christ shows some help for His church / everything is silent among preachers and rulers; and they do not want the King in Zion. What wonder is it / that the children cry out to God / and must become our teachers? because we do not take it to heart /

<column-break/>

but also grumble about it / as the Jews once did over the children's <term>Hosanna</term> / so Christ might be crucified in His members / and Jerusalem's downfall might be near.

§. 30. And so that everyone can see / what kind of prayers the Silesian children pray / I will add some here. 

> Holy / righteous / and true God! You are not a God / who is pleased by godless behavior / but You hate and punish the same earnestly / even unto the third and fourth generation! can anything / O righteous Lord / anger You more violently / than the base contempt of Your holy Word / sacraments / and all faithful prayers and ministers: for the sake of such contempt and sin / You allow people to fall into powerful errors. You indeed wipe out entire kingdoms / principalities / and lands / as has been experienced with the greatest harm / especially the first world <note>referring to the world before the Biblical Great Flood</note> / the city of Jerusalem / and the Jewish people. Behold! we cannot keep our sin secret / but must freely and publicly confess / admit and say: We have sinned along with our fathers / we have acted wrongly / and have been godless. Out of pure goodness / You have drawn us out of dark paganism / and brought us to the knowledge of You / and from one time to another / You have given us the Word with great crowds of <term>evangelists</term> <note>here meaning those who preach the Gospel</note>: But alas! we have thanked You little for it / praised You poorly for the revelation of Your Gospel / but rather abandoned Your Word /

->4.<-

Yet if, because of our guilt,
You wish to leave us still in hunger <note>In this context, hunger refers to "spiritual famine"—the lack of access to the Word and Sacraments in their own churches.</note>;
Then grant that we, in patience,
Resign ourselves to your will!
If your hour is not yet here,

->5.<-

In the meantime, let us continually
Walk in your ways,
And diligently practice your word, full of grace,
In our homes! <note>An allusion to "house churches" or private family worship, which was the only way for many Protestants to practice their faith when public worship was banned.</note>
Ah! Dwell within our souls,
And let them be your temples;

->6.<-

Now, dear God, we wish to
Hand this work entirely over to you;
Do what you will, help me, that we
May live according to your will!
Keep us always in peace and rest,
Cast all your blessing upon us,
And help us poor children!

Another prayer has come into my hands, which is better expressed in various parts, and reads as follows: O you eternal, Merciful God and Father, see, we poor, unworthy, forsaken children come before your most holy face, in quite simple

---

assembly, at your command, and in the name of your Son, who commanded the little children to come to him, and rejects no one who truly comes. Though we should rightly be afraid because of our weakness to appear before you, Majestic God—you who are lauded and praised with a strong voice and great spirit by the thrones, and dominions, and principalities in heaven—yet, because we remember that you have prepared a power for yourself out of the mouths of young children and infants <note>A reference to Psalm 8:2, a central text for the children's prayer movement to justify their public activity.</note>, through which the "strongly-armed one" and the "man of sin and perdition" <note>original: Mensch der Sünden; a common Reformation-era term for the Antichrist, often associated with the Papacy by Protestants of this time.</note> shall be exterminated and destroyed, we come in comforted confidence and do not cast away our trust, even though we are yet so weak in ourselves. Instead, we believe that you will succeed in these days with the children of men, in that you grant so much holy incense <note>original: Rauch-Werck; referring to the prayers of the faithful ascending like incense.</note> for the prayer of all saints in so many places here and there, and through such precursors, you hasten with no slow steps toward your coming <note>original: Zukunfft; referring to the Second Coming of Christ or a divine intervention.</note>. Ah Lord! Decorate us then yourself with the adornment of your Son, and give us much of his Spirit, so that we may watch in prayer, lest the invisible enemies outwit us, nor our own seductive heart deceive us. You must do it, O God, within us, because we [can do] nothing...

### §. 31.

I could add various other prayers / if they were not too long-winded / and since it is not necessary / we shall turn to their judgment <note>original: "Judicio"</note> and opinion / and see / how they so furiously and powerfully opposed this blameless work of the children. Here appears the written circular of Mr. M. David Schindler, Head Pastor <note>original: "Paſtoris Primarii"</note> and President <note>original: "Präfidis"</note> of the Evangelical schools in Liegnitz <note>Modern-day Legnica, Poland.</note>, addressed to the neighboring clergy regarding the containment of the influx of children to the extraordinary prayer / which reads thus: "That through a visible cloak—

<meta>catchword: tel (cloak)</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>141</page-num>
<header>(0)</header>

,,—of piety <note>original: "Pietät"</note>, much damage is inflicted upon the Christian Church / as the history of many centuries <note>original: "Seculis"</note> abundantly proves. For under this appearance / and beautiful form / many kinds of hypocrisy <note>original: "Gleiſſnereyen"</note> have gradually and unnoticed crept into religion itself; / and where such errors were perhaps not allowed to rage publicly / they were hidden under the respectable appearance of good ceremonies / and penetrated into the hearts of God-fearing people. The examples are common / and such evils lie plainly enough before our eyes / having sprung from this evil world / which cannot be described with ink / nor sufficiently wept over with tears; indeed / according to the agreement of a famous theologian / no greater damage was ever brought upon the Church through the cruelty of tyrants than through the secret appearance of the fear of God. What I mean by this / you will no doubt notice. Namely, a new devotion / a so-called enthusiasm <note>original: "Enthuſiaſmus"; in the 18th century, this term was a pejorative meaning fanatical or delusional religious fervor.</note> has come upon the tender youth / so that they are not satisfied with their fathers' houses / schools / and finally the church itself / but run in crowds to the open field / and there / under the open sky / regardless of the storm / hold prayer hours with special prayer formulas—composed I know not from where—to petition the God who is angered by our sins for the general welfare. It has a great appearance of the fear of God / and seems almost beneficial to the common good / especially at this—

must withdraw it from the **Ministry** <note>original: "Ministerio"; the collective body of ordained clergy</note> / so that many of them / are not exposed / and shamed / because they do not pray as diligently / and as persistently as the children. The true servants of the **Gospel** <note>original: "Evangelii"</note> however, rejoice / and lift their hands with them up to **GOD** / and recognize from such signs of these times / that something great is imminent for the Church and the government / and that it is indeed necessary / that one encourages everyone to prayer / and does not hold them back from it. Therefore, the boast of the Mr. Pastor / and **Senior** <note>original: "Senioris"; a high-ranking elder or senior clergyman</note> of the churches and schools is not his own / that he would have been minded of his own accord / to put a stop to the miraculous prayer of the **Silesian Children** / if the **Liegnitz Government** <note>The Duchy of Liegnitz (Legnica) was a center of this prayer movement</note> had not already done so. Mr. Schindler is again fearful at the wrong time / and says / would not this gathering / under whatever name of godliness it might be hidden / turn into **wantonness** / and other evil customs? As if it would certainly happen / by some necessary consequence / that mischief must awaken from it / when experience has taught the exact opposite. For just as the dear children come together in the highest order / and love / and close their circle of love with their hands joined together / and then fall upon their faces / or perform their prayer kneeling / so they also depart from one another pair by pair / as **Mr. M. Scharff** <note>Magister Gottfried Scharff, a contemporary chronicler of the movement</note> himself reports / and show no wantonness at all / nor other evil thoughts

---

<page-num>147</page-num>
<header>❧ (0) ❧</header>

thoughts / which do not befit Christian children. It is otherwise quite true / as Mr. Schindler also cites in his letters to the priesthood / that children are very inclined toward misuse / and vice; again, it is true / that these youths can hardly be tamed by their **teachers** <note>original: "Præceptoribus"</note> and parents / but this very fact works against him / as visible evidence has shown / how these otherwise **unruly** <note>original: "rohe"; literally "raw" or unrefined</note> children kept themselves in order / without any compulsion from the teachers / and parents / which requires a higher hand / and indicates / that the **Holy Spirit** must have worked all of this in the hearts of the children / who has restrained all misuse / and vice / to which they are otherwise naturally inclined / and has made them pious, obedient children. The Mr. Senior further writes on the one hand / how he finds no suspicion of deceit at all among these youth / as if they sought only to enchant the minds of men through some apparent hidden cunning / and seek to introduce something evil into the church. On the other hand, however / he attributes to them an <term>enthusiasm</term> <note>original: "Enthuſiaſmum"; in this period, this was a derogatory term for religious fanaticism or claiming direct inspiration from God outside of church authority.</note> / and claims that they are not satisfied with their fathers' houses and schools / and finally not even with the church itself / and run out into the field. How does that agree? To say / that the children are not satisfied with the church itself / and yet again to say of them / that they do not seek to introduce evil into the church? It is publicly clear / that the dear children / who know nothing of **enthusiasm** / turn their hearts to **GOD** in simplicity / and [pray] for the turning away...

prayer of the children be hindered / and they follow

He criticizes church officials who try to suppress these gatherings, suggesting that the children's prayers are a defense against God's wrath rather than a threat to the church.</summary>

be; [those] who have also prayed kneeling under the open sky. These same ones often perform their prayer by compulsion; the children in Silesia, however, do so in a voluntary movement, upon whom the Lord our God will yet have mercy, just as He had mercy on the great city of **Nineveh** for the sake of the 120,000 people who did not know the difference between what is right or left. I have written this out of love for the truth to the **Mr. Senior and President** <note>original: "Seniori und præsidi"</note>, so that he may not oppose the work of **GOD**, which He has done and still does through the children; but rather that he, with his **fellow ministers** <note>original: "Comministerialen"</note>, may bow their knees before **GOD** and, with a joined hand, pray for themselves, for their congregations, and for the whole **Silesian Land**, together with the children, and be heard.

§. 33. Beside the **judgment** <note>original: "Judicio"</note> of the **Senior from Liegnitz** <note>original: "Lignitzischen"</note>, Mr. **Christian Sommer**, Pastor and Senior of the clergy in **Oels** <note>original: "Olß"</note>, has judged thus:
> This new, childish prayer-devotion, which has arisen here and there—yet is not commanded by a single word in Scripture, nor confirmed by any example—no one will approve; rather, I have held it back in this place from the beginning, but on the contrary have faithfully impressed the manner and way prescribed by **Christ** in **Matthew 6**. But I ask also: where does it stand forbidden in the **Holy Scripture**? Rather, it is confirmed by the fact that just as men **in every place** <note>Greek: "ἐν παντὶ τόπῳ"</note> shall pray and lift up holy hands without wrath,

<page-num>151</page-num>
<header>-*-(0)-*- 151</header>

and without **doubting** <note>Greek: "διαλογισμῷ", from 1 Timothy 2:8; refers to inward disputing or hesitation</note>, so also the children have to follow them in this, for they are the most capable [of it], without such **doubting**, as they do not have much **reasoning** <note>original: "raisonirens"; here meaning intellectualizing or over-thinking</note>, [but] to lift their hands to **GOD** to pray in all places, even on the field under the open sky, as **Christ the Lord** often did; and for this He chose not the **Temple in Jerusalem**—though it was built by **GOD'S** command—but the mountains and the fields for prayer. We also have an example in **Moses**, who, when **Israel** fought against the **Amalekites**, went with **Aaron** and **Hur** to the top of the mountain and prayed there with the lifting of his hands to **GOD**. This was actually the office of **Aaron**, who dealt with prayer and sacrifice; but in **extraordinary** things, **GOD** prays through whom He wills. Since the Lord has now done something special with the **Silesian children**, who will prevent Him from going into the field with them and preparing a power for Himself through the young children and infants? Instead of **Mr. Sommer** upholding and supporting their hands, as **Aaron** and **Hur** did for **Moses**—that is, putting his own hands to such prayer and work—behold, it is they who seek to hinder and hold it back in all sorts of ways; which truly is not fine, and will not give them a good name among the faithful. commanded or recommended, reports, and has faithfully impressed this upon his congregation, he has not done wrong in that, if only he had not thereby sought to overturn the extraordinary public prayer of the children. For just as they themselves, the priests, do not wish to imply that public prayers in the church are forbidden by **Christ** because of private chamber-prayer, but can quite well reconcile the two with one another, so one cannot cite that same prayer of the children—whom **GOD** has chosen and used as public persons in an extraordinary way against the lethargy of so many priests and others—against the saying of **Christ** from the **VI. Chapter of Matthew**, nor claim it to be contrary to it. The preacher in **Kunitz**, Mr. **Johan Christian Bauch**, has given the following brief <term>decision <note>Latin "decisum"; a formal legal or theological judgment</note></term>: "This prayer-assembly and this man's word was childish; therefore this childish devotion has also come to a childish end." But this devotion of the children has not yet come to an end to this very hour, as I have seen with my own eyes and recently heard with my own ears—what sort of inward devotion they showed, and how they sang so fervently and heartily from their childlike hearts; therefore, this prayer-assembly is not as childish as he judges it to be. His hasty judging is the work of men, and his conclusion is childish. Oh, how well it would stand if an <term>inspector <note>A high-ranking church official responsible for supervising clergy</note></term>, or some other

individual priests became united among themselves, and came together in the church, or elsewhere as they wished, and prayed as these children in **Silesia** are here showing them the way; that would please **GOD** better than shameful vice. Samuel Keiper** at **Heinerdorf** judges thus: "Because this childish devotion seems not only to end in wantonness, but also to turn to the detriment of public worship, I have first of all forbidden it." But no wantonness was perceived in the praying children; rather, through such prayer, they turned from wanton children into obedient ones, and gladly submitted to the teaching of their <term>teachers <note>Latin "Præceptoren"; refers to tutors or schoolmasters</note></term> during their prayer, whereas previously they were brought to such things only with great difficulty; therefore, it is easy to prove against this Mr. <term>Pastor <note>original: "Paſtorem"</note></term> that it was not as childish as he calls such devotion with mocking words. If only the good gentlemen had waited a while and watched for a bit to see if this devotion resulted in wantonness, they would have acted more wisely and prudently; but now they generally speak as they do, saying that the childish devotion is accustomed to end in wantonness and to turn to the detriment of public worship—which, however, to date has happened just as little as the previous things they feared. Therefore, it is no argument at all by which he could have been moved to first [forbid] it in his place.

Mr. *Johan Christoffer Möller*, preacher at **Roiz**, lets this matter depend upon the government's prohibition and wishes to simultaneously admonish his listeners toward an orderly and seemly devotion. work likely stems from the Swedish gentlemen <note>original: "Herrn Schweden"; likely a reference to the Swedish soldiers who, during their military campaigns in Germany, were known for holding public prayers in the field.</note> who, as they were accustomed to holding their devotions on the march in the fields, moved the children to mimic them. Both of these judgments are very unkind. Firstly, Mr. Gerlach calls the gatherings of the praying children an "evil habit." How can it be an evil habit when young people—who are generally very sluggish toward prayer—are now driven toward prayer against their usual nature? If it is <term>enthusiasm<gloss>In this period, "enthusiasm" was a derogatory term used by the established church to describe religious fanaticism or the claim of direct divine inspiration.</gloss></term>, then the children in the Temple at Jerusalem, when they shouted hosannas to **Christ**, must also have possessed an enthusiasm; for that was also a completely extraordinary work, which seemed just as strange to the priests of that time as it does to those today. Yet the **Lord JESUS** refuted them with the eighth Psalm, and His Spirit does the same now, justifying the children He loves so much against those who speak against them. In the second judgment of Gerlach (whether he be the same man or another), it is scolded as a <term>Pietistic<gloss>A movement within Lutheranism emphasizing personal devotion and "heart-religion" over strict doctrinal conformity.</gloss></term> or <term>Quakerish<gloss>A reference to the Religious Society of Friends; at this time, "Quaker" was often used as an insult to imply that someone was a religious radical or a heretic.</gloss></term> nature, and even attributed to an evil spirit. What must the poor, innocent children think when they read such an evil judgment? For when they hear that their prayer—which they are convinced pleases **GOD**—is called "Quakerish," they might easily come to the thought that the Quakers might not be such evil people after all, being compared to children who are conscious of no evil, and that the Quakers might be treated just as unjustly as the children 

<meta>catchword: themselves</meta>

themselves are. The children in the Temple at Jerusalem also did not possess such external, <term>dogmatic<gloss>Related to the formal, rigid teachings and doctrines of the church.</gloss></term> knowledge, and yet they received the testimony from **Christ** that they had truly praised Him, and that He had "prepared a power out of their mouths" to destroy the enemy and the vengeful. But as for the Pastor putting the Quakers and the Pietists in a parallel, he acts as a public slanderer. Let him show such a parallelism between those who are called Pietists and the Quakers, which will be difficult for him to do. And who has taken a name that is good in itself—derived from "piety"—and turned it into a sect, other than those who were not pious themselves, and who caused a division and devised a new nickname? Godliness is neither enthusiasm nor Pietism, but belongs to all people and has the promise of this life and the next. Whoever would call himself a "Pietist" is not truly pious, but seeks something through such words; but we, who believe we are made righteous through the **Lord JESUS** and through His grace,

-> ❧ (0) ❧ <-

...persevere / and the **Lord** / when He comes / may find us not sleeping / but awake.

§. 37. Since the watchmen <note>referring to church leaders or civil authorities who should have been spiritually alert</note> among the people / alas! / have fallen asleep / **God** has awakened the children / so that through this innocent age / and through their bowed knees we might learn to bow before His great Majesty / who has indeed already shown the rider on the red horse in the second seal / as he has actively galloped about in war / and has taken peace from the earth / which is followed by the well-known plagues of the land / hunger and pestilence <note>original Greek: λιμὸς and λοιμὸς</note> / just as it follows in the Holy Revelation / specifically in the third and fourth seals one after another. It is not without reason what those have said who consider and view the prayer of the children as something Divine / that nevertheless no one commanded the children to pray / since the natural naughtiness / especially among the common folk in the countryside / is for the most part such that they must be driven to prayer / and that such a work could increase so quickly in five days in five Principalities <note>Historical administrative regions in Silesia</note> / such as in **Liegnitz**, **Jauer**, **Glogau**, **Schweidnitz**, and **Wohlau** / even where the march of the Swedes did not reach / that such foreign children <note>children from outside the immediate movement's origin</note> immediately surrendered their will and spirit to prayer / and held fast to it. Conversely / the comparison to "room arrest" <note>being kept indoors during the cold months</note> / which children must keep in winter / and who come out with their toys in hand at the onset

<column-break/>

of spring / does not apply / since they are by nature inclined to that / but not to prayer / which is very difficult for them / and toward which they are very sluggish. Likewise / the proverb "**A child is a credulous thing**" <note>original Latin: *Credula res Puer est*, a quote from Ovid's *Heroides* often cited in scholarly works of this era</note> / which is cited from Camerarius / does not suffice / because children are indeed prone to adopt something quickly / but also to leave it again just as quickly / especially when it becomes difficult for them. Here / however / they have remained alert for prayer / and could be held back from it neither by winter / nor by snow and rain / but rather took a little straw with them / laid it under their knees / and knelt upon it / which truly indicates a higher pull / which drew them so powerfully to it / and has preserved them in such constancy until this day / in so many places. The disposition of the praying Swedish soldiers has already been dealt with above / to which I refer Mr. Magister Scharf. But has not the highly renowned Saxon theologian Dr. Neumann <note>Caspar Neumann (1648–1715), a famous hymn writer and theologian</note> / among other beautiful words in the **introductory program to the Malmberg dissertation** <note>original: *Programmate Inaugurali dissertationi Malmbergianæ præmisso*</note> regarding the Swedish army entering Saxony—which was certainly not such a welcome messenger to Wittenberg and the Professors there—been able to boast and say: That one could have seen nothing more devout than a David of our times <note>A comparison of King Charles XII of Sweden to the biblical King David</note> beginning and completing the march with prayer and singing / how much more then should the Silesian preachers prove their oratory in this / that

that one could have seen nothing better or more devout than the **Jerusalem-children** <note>A reference to the children who greeted Jesus with "Hosanna" in Jerusalem; here used to describe the contemporary praying children</note> of our times meeting the approaching **King of Honor** with praying and singing, and wishing Him luck with a joyful **Hosanna**. But because this did not happen, our cold-heartedness has been noticed, not without shame. It is indeed argued that if the children in **Silesia** had begun to pray in such a manner before the **Swedes** <note>The Swedish army under Charles XII, who were involved in the Great Northern War and occupied parts of Saxony and Silesia</note> had entered the land, or had preceded them with their prayer, one would gladly recognize it as something entirely miraculous; but then people would have found something else to object to. However, it is significant enough that, as is known from the above account, the children had already built a church before the **Swedes** had even moved into **Saxony** with their army, which could certainly have been taken for a very meaningful omen. Truly, as the **Mr. Pastor** believes, the desire to be rid of the book or the **spinning wheel** <note>The tools of schoolwork and domestic labor</note> for an hour did not drive the children to this; for they do not refuse to do their work—whereby the presumed excuses of laziness are sufficiently refuted—but rather the impulse to prayer was so great that no hardship of the storm, nor the anger of parents and **preceptors** <note>original Latin: præceptoren; teachers or tutors</note>, could keep them from it. What sorts of things are devised by those who have once set their minds to making the prayer of these **innocents** <note>literally "those not yet of age"</note> suspicious and to reject-

<column-break/>

-ing it. Such things are also sought out in the tenth and eleventh **paragraphs**, but in vain. The children do not allow themselves to be led astray in their simple, faithful prayer; rather, they rightly answer their adversaries that since the ministers themselves had so often urged prayer in their sermons, their discouragement now cannot seem anything but quite strange to the children when they now do what they were taught, but must experience that the ministers are not satisfied with it. The preachers should know that their single **Abba** <note>Aramaic for "Father," used here to denote an intimate, heartfelt cry to God</note>, spoken in a childlike heart, will be more pleasing to **God** than the artificial and formulaic babble of falsely-celebrated art, which is an abomination before **God**, who therefore does not wish to smell <note>A biblical idiom meaning God rejects the "aroma" or spiritual quality of their worship</note> their assemblies where things proceed so coldly. They, the children, have answered the opposers very well: that praying is indeed something good, and one should not forbid them from the prayer which they perform out of such good impulse. It is not merely a "good intention" among them—which, to be sure, does not immediately justify a thing—but it is a heartfelt earnestness and a fitting, godly manner, which one can also perform outside of the churches in the open fields, and which pleases the **LORD**, who is present everywhere, and leaves a blessing behind. Let them build a church in the spirit there, which pleases the **Lord** better <note>original: "baß"</note> than if even the finest churches were built and decorated with many images.

§. 38. They, the children, have also offered the **prayers** and the **hymns**—which were so well-suited to the prayers and the season—with such devotion that I was astonished when I heard it and perceived that the **precentor** <term>Vorsänger; the leader of congregational singing</term> must not have been without understanding of the matter, and that he, as well as the others, well knew and understood what they sang. Should there be some among the little ones who might not have such understanding, it is nevertheless enough that they understand something of it according to their own measure. Should one go to the churches over which our **opponents** are placed, and conduct a **visitation** <term>An official inspection of a parish by church authorities to assess the spiritual state and knowledge of the congregation</term> with the adults, and ask them what was sung and prayed, or what was taught in the sermon, what errors and ignorance would one not find among them? Yet these are admitted to **Confession** and the **Holy Communion**, as the Lord’s Table, which they ought rightly to discern before they are admitted. But there one has enough excuses at hand so that such ignorant people are pardoned, and so much is overlooked; here, however, one is so sharp and so discerning toward the children—here one is such a **precisist** <note>A legalist or someone who insists on extreme, literal correctness</note> and wishes to demand so much from them, only so that everything may be sought out to abolish and frustrate the **child-prayer** <note>original: Kinder-Gebeth; referring to the controversial spontaneous prayer meetings held by children in fields and open spaces</note> which does not sit well with us. For me, it is a settled matter that those who encourage the praying children in their prayer and defend it have a better cause before **GOD** than those who [seek to abolish it] either through power and force, or in a cunning and—

---

<page-num>183</page-num>

**way** <note>catchword: Weiße/way</note> gradually wish to see it abolished, among which latter group **Mr. M. Scharffeiner** is included. If only the prayer is brought to a standstill and abolished, it is all the same whether it happens through force or through cunning; both parties have one **goal** and one **effect**, though by different paths and plots. Indeed, I can say that just as the tyrants of the Church of God have not been able to do as much harm as the secret and domestic **heretics** and **hypocrites** who deceive souls and lead them away from Christ under the appearance of justice and truth; so too is the secret and cunning **method** <note>original: Modus</note>, by which the children are gradually drawn away from prayer in a flattering manner and outwitted by the enchanting chatter of tongues, much more harmful than all external force, which they would gladly endure, suffering something for Christ’s sake. In contrast, through a cunning persuasion **by the trickery of men, in the cunning craftiness of deceitful plotting** <note>original Greek: ἐν κυβείᾳ τῶν ἀνθρώπων, ἐν πανουργίᾳ πρὸς τὴν μεθοδείαν τῆς πλάνης</note>, the tender minds of the children are diverted and led away from their good goal and earnestness, and from the spirit of their good will. **Ephesians 4:14**. And this is the aim of the **counsel** <note>original: Consilium</note> of the author, where he advises that one must not show oneself too strict against the children's simplicity, but must admit that, if the children must always be occupied with something, this (namely, prayer) is certainly a better game than if they undertook something vain or entirely evil; yet one must deal with the praying children... **gent-** <note>catchword: lich/ly; from 'glimpfflich' (gently/mildly)</note>

...could nevertheless be added; there were even so several circumstances because of which it would now be more convenient and better that they cease these public gatherings they had made for themselves. There, God would hear them just as gladly and just as easily, since a righteous prayer penetrates through all walls even into the clouds. In this case, as in others, they should follow sensible people who see further than their childhood allows, and who have valid reasons for their prohibition—reasons which the children's simplicity could not quite reach. But the best part is that such simplicity does not understand this **serpent-like wisdom** <note>A reference to Matthew 10:16, used here pejoratively to describe the clever but manipulative arguments of the clergy</note> and holds fast to that which they have felt in their hearts through the Spirit of God. They know how well they feel when they pray and have prayed, and how they have grown in God—a growth that was previously very poor, as their parents and **teachers** <term>original: *Præceptores*; the Latin term for private tutors or schoolmasters</term> and many others recognize and can sufficiently testify.

One sees, however, the cunning schemes and with what a facade of piety this priest wanted to persuade the children—even by misusing Scripture—claiming that the obedience of children is their most beautiful ornament and better than sacrifice, ***1 Samuel 15:22***. Yet these very priests should themselves show obedience to God and not resist Him; for He intended to set up the praying children like a torch against the lukewarmness and sloth

They took the raw meat with the fat by force out of the hands of the people offering sacrifices, and moreover, they slept in a shameful manner with the women who served at the door of the **Tabernacle** <note>The "Hütten des Stifts" refers to the portable dwelling place of God's presence in the Old Testament</note>. Therefore I have sworn to the house of **Eli** that the **iniquity** of Eli’s house shall not be atoned for with sacrifice or offering forever."

These are certainly harsh words to me: that the **iniquity** of the house of **Eli** (which serves as a prototype and image of the corrupt state of the Church and the ministers of the Church in all times) shall not be atoned for with sacrifice or offering forever, because such sins, which are committed with...

<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of the "praying children" movement (Kinderbeten) in 18th-century Silesia. ...let the intercession <note>original: *Vorbitte*</note> of Jesus Christ, our Reconciler and Advocate, benefit us. Who would here presume to turn the grace of God into wantonness? Or use it as a cloak of excuse to strengthen that which is wrong? Far be it from us. We only affirm that the weaknesses of children do not make their prayers go unheard; rather, even the babbling of children (of which we have already made mention above) is pleasing to the Lord God. We also affirm that their prayers, though still imperfect, are nevertheless worthy and acceptable to both men and angels.

The Reverend Pastor, in section 14 on page 19, does not wish to be seen as a despiser of children’s prayer, and therefore brings forward at length much that might serve as a **commendation** <note>original: *Commendation*; a formal praise or recommendation</note> of children, who are also members of the Christian Church. However, his **protest is contrary to his actions** <note>original: *protestatio ist Contraria facto*</note>, in that his advice actually aims to use "polite manners" and "nimble caution" to effectively drive the children away from their usual prayer gatherings and to disturb them.

If it is true—as it indeed is, by his own admission—that the prayers of children are the best and often the most acceptable to God; if children have not burdened their hearts with images, vain things, and worries about their livelihood as the elders have; and if they are furthermore not so contrary to God through so many sins as the elders and adults are, because their hearts are purer and more simple; and if for that reason

<column-break/>

they are so dear and acceptable to God and Christ that He bids them come to Him and punishes those who would prevent it—yes, even He who has ordained a power out of their mouths to destroy the enemy and the vengeful—ah! why then is one so troubled? Why does one struggle so much against it and even write a treatise against it?

If it is true—as it indeed is, as he writes on page 21—that the children of the heathens touched the heart of our God, and that when a great need faces the Church or otherwise looms, it is commanded that one should gather the children and infants together? And that beautiful song:

> O great God of praise, when the measure of sins is full and You wish to destroy us in Your wrath, the little children might yet do Your right will; therefore, You would spare us and not reward us according to our deeds,

which a certain teacher of our Church could never sing without anguish and tears? And **Luther** <note>Martin Luther (1483–1546), the primary figure of the Protestant Reformation</note> said very well that "we elders eat with the children, and not they with us," thereby indicating with what **respect** <note>original: *Respect*</note> and with what entirely different eyes he viewed children. His **comrade** <note>original: *Commilito*; literally a "fellow soldier" or close colleague</note> **Master Melanchthon** <note>Philipp Melanchthon (1497–1560), a collaborator with Luther</note> did the same, and during the troubled course of the Reformation, he once found himself particularly uplifted by the prayer of children.

Furthermore, in beloved **Silesia** <note>A historical region in Central Europe, mostly in modern-day Poland</note> at **Lauban** <note>Now Lubań, Poland</note>, a praiseworthy custom is cited by the Reverend Pastor: that in certain cases involving the children’s school-prayer, the need and the concerns...
