...allow to be included; how then does he come to the point that he takes such pains to disturb and abolish these Silesian praying children <note>The "Silesian Praying Children" was a movement in 1707–1708 where thousands of children gathered in fields to pray for religious freedom, often without adult supervision.</note> in their prayer, while they pray so fervently and innocently for the distress of the whole Church? Does he wish to object that prayer in and of itself is not forbidden, but rather the question is whether, in these times and in our land, children should and may organize such self-authorized <note>original: *eigen mächtige*</note> public prayer hours everywhere on their own? And whether they should not rather seize the opportunity to call upon the Most High often and much in silence, for themselves, and without such an attention-grabbing display? 

But he may dispute this with the One who gave them such an impulse for prayer at this very time, and specifically in the land of Silesia; just as that same **God** has likewise awakened such children in other places, such as in France and England <note>This likely refers to the "French Prophets" or Camisard movement and similar child-prophet phenomena in the late 17th century.</note>, so that they began such an extraordinary work <note>original: *extraordinaires Werck*</note> and still continue with it. When it shall please **God**—after they have testified long enough against the sluggish world through such a remarkable deed and unusual movement—to bring the children back into silence, He will certainly see to His own timing for it. Those who are true children of **God** move when the cloud of **God** <note>A biblical reference to the pillar of cloud that guided the Israelites in the wilderness (Exodus 13:21).</note> moves and goes before them, and they rest again when it rests. Therefore, my well-intended advice to the Magister would be that he remember the words of the beloved Bridegroom of the soul <note>A common Pietist term for Jesus Christ.</note>, the heavenly Solomon, when

<meta>catchword: he</meta>

---

<page-num>191</page-num>
<header>* (0) *</header>

he says with great emphasis in the Song of Solomon <note>original: *hohen Liede*</note> three times, namely in the second chapter verse 7, chapter 3, and chapter 8 verse 4:
> I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, that you stir not up my love, nor awake it, until it pleases itself.

And he, conversely, through this my adjuration, should in no way disturb the praying children from their public field-prayers <note>original: *Feld-Gebet*</note> until it pleases them to stop on their own. These so-called "self-authorized" public prayer hours of his come from the power that **God** the **Lord**—whose name we shall see become glorious in all lands—has prepared for Himself in little children and infants. The Pastor, with all his power and cunning, cautious schemes, cannot disturb this, and it may not be taken away from them according to Christian liberty.

### §. 40.
Mr. **Magister Scharff** <note>Gottfried Balthasar Scharff (1676–1744), a Lutheran theologian and critic of the children's movement.</note> wishes in section 17 to injure the right of spiritual freedom <note>original: *geistliche Freyheit*</note>, upon which the blessed **Luther** so strongly insisted and upheld. He wishes to dispute with the children whether they are free to hold such prayer as they have begun in the field, under the open sky. By virtue of such freedom, **Luther** rid himself of all papal external regulations when they tried to make a necessity of them, even though he accommodated <note>original: *accommodiret*</note> them for a considerable time as long as his conscience allowed it; but then they became weary of him, declared him a heretic, and cast him out of their church. I know

He invokes the concept of "spiritual freedom" championed by Luther to argue that these children should not be forced into silence by religious authorities.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of the "Praying Children" movement in Silesia (1707–1708). The author argues against Lutheran authorities who sought to suppress the movement using old church laws, asserting that these spontaneous gatherings are a matter of Christian freedom and divine impulse rather than a violation of ecclesiastical order.</meta>

<page-num>192</page-num>
<header>(0)</header>

...not what the Papal <note>Roman Catholic</note> Church would have done and still does—she who knows so much to say about obedience to the Church—if such children had arisen among them who had begun in such an unusual way, of their own accord, to pray with such constancy and fervor. But behold, the Evangelicals <note>In this context, the Lutheran established church.</note> only want to make a coercion of conscience out of it, and quench the Spirit with force, and terrify them where **GOD** and the child’s heart will not allow themselves to be terrified. The cited *Liegnitz* <note>original: *Lignitischen*</note> Church Ordinance, which was reintroduced and printed for the second time by princely command in the year 1594, cannot be drawn here as proof, because it had not yet experienced such a nature of the case <note>original: *Speciem Facti*</note> and therefore could not have made any regulation. Yet it says enough: that Christian freedom consists, among other things, in not allowing oneself to be held captive by ceremonies <note>original: *Ceremonien*</note> ordained by men; much less could it have commanded that if something extraordinary <note>original: *extraordinaires*</note> and quite unusual manifests in the Church, one should hinder it in any way <note>original: *quovis modo*</note> and set oneself against it. They could therefore neither ordain anything positive regarding the praying children, nor could they have foreseen that something of the sort would happen. If they assert in the Church Ordinance that it is **GOD's** will that His Gospel be preached publicly, and that He Himself should have honorable assemblies for that purpose, this may [not] serve as an argument against the assemblies of the children...

<column-break/>

<page-num>193</page-num>
<header>(0)</header>
...since they have not yet undertaken to act as teachers and preach publicly. Assemblies should ordinarily be held in the churches built for that purpose; but that does not cancel things out if a Christian teacher wished to establish a private exercise of godliness <note>original: *Privat-Ubung der Gottseeligkeit*</note> and give freedom there to the people—who are still ashamed to ask questions in the large congregation—to discuss their doubts <note>original: *Dubia*</note> or otherwise contribute what serves for edification from the presented text of Holy Scripture. Likewise, no private citizen is forbidden by the right of Christian freedom from holding a home-exercise and conversation about **GOD** and His Word with his household members, or even calling his neighbor and his friends to join. Would to **GOD** that He would grant many like Eldad and Medad <note>Biblical figures from Numbers 11:26-29 who received the Spirit and prophesied in the camp rather than the Tabernacle; Moses famously refused to stop them.</note>, and that men would subject themselves in humble obedience to the Spirit of **GOD**, who so gladly wishes to teach them; then things would look better in the Church than they do now. Those who were true preachers and servants of **GOD** would also greatly rejoice over it; but if those who seek their own interests and not what is of **JESUS CHRIST** look askance at it and grumble against it, so that they may retain their monopoly <note>original: *Monopolium*</note> and rule over consciences, then let them do so; but they shall not rob or take from us our freedom with which **CHRIST** has set us free. What is cited from Augustine’s Letter 118 to Januarius <note>A letter by St. Augustine of Hippo concerning the variety of local church customs and the importance of not imposing unnecessary burdens on the faithful.</note> also belongs here...

He compares the children's spontaneous gatherings to private home worship and the biblical examples of Eldad and Medad, accusing critical clergy of trying to maintain a "monopoly" over religious life.</summary>

...from this. It is one thing for a preacher who comes to a foreign place and finds certain *ceremonies* <note>original: *Ceremonien*</note> there which the church has used previously and which, as free <term>indifferent matters</term> <note>original: *Mitteldinge*, also known as Adiaphora; practices that are neither commanded nor forbidden by God.</note> can quite well be retained, to then in such a *case* <note>original: *Casu*</note> follow the custom of the church, rather than the church following the preacher and having to adopt something new every time a new one arrives. We certainly approve of this and hold to it with **St. Augustine**. 

But it is quite another thing whether someone who is *extraordinarily* <note>original: *extraordinaire*</note> driven to something by the **Spirit of GOD** should flatly discontinue the same for the sake of another’s opinion—especially since the one who has such an *extraordinary* drive for good does not *disturb* <note>original: *turbiret*</note> the church or the church order at all, nor intends to *disturb* <note>original: *turbiren*</note> it. Since the prayer of the **Silesian children** is good, and such an unusual drive for it is found in them contrary to their [childish] nature, neither the church nor a **parish priest** <note>original: *Parochus*</note> is authorized to hinder it—though they certainly would be if something should occur among them that rightfully should be punished and forbidden. 

The verses cited by the **Pastor**, **Romans 14:19** and **Galatians 5:13**, are *irrelevant* <note>original: *impertinenter*</note> to this matter. Through their prayer, the children strive so that they and the whole land might become better; they come together quite peacefully, leaving the preachers their churches in peace, and even attend them. But when the poor children—who know, and have often heard from the pulpit, 

<column-break/>

<page-num>195</page-num>
<header>-❀(0)❀- 195</header>

that they should pray diligently—must experience that those very people who praised it to them in the sermon are themselves against it, they are thereby made to stumble. Thus, the preachers themselves act against this verse and do not strive for what serves peace and improvement. 

Even if the children were to give "room to the flesh" through their freedom, that would be an abuse of freedom; but as it is, they entreat **GOD** in their prayer that they may be loosed from sin and become the Lord’s freemen—that is, servants of righteousness. Why then are they criticized? And why does one start such discord in the church? 

**In summary:** Public and general prayer hours <note>original: *Beth-Stunden*</note> **Note well:** ordinarily belong in the House of the Lord and primarily at the time which the whole church has designated for them. On the other hand, however, public and general prayer hours can be held *extraordinarily* in other places as well—as is happening with these children in the fields—by those who are extraordinarily awakened to it by **GOD**. 

When **GOD the Lord** in the Old Testament appointed regular priests in the Temple at Jerusalem, all those who belonged there had to adhere to it; but when He awakened extraordinary **prophets**—for example, **Amos** the cowherd—who had to confront the people with their sins at **GOD's** command, **GOD** always had the free power to do so. This is by the power of His witnessing word in the final time, when the **Antichrist**, the enemy, and the covetous shall be destroyed; at which time...

He accuses the clergy of hypocrisy for telling children to pray and then punishing them when they do so spontaneously in the fields.</summary>

> In my humble opinion, the undertaking of our praying children can be pleasing neither to God in Heaven, nor to the authorities on Earth, nor to any enlightened **member** <term>Gliedmasse</term> <note>Literally "limb," referring to an individual part of the spiritual "Body of Christ" or the Church.</note> of the Christian orthodox church.

If it is indeed as he reports here—that the prayer of the **Silesian children** <note>The Silesian Children's Prayer Movement (Kinderbeten) of 1707-1708 involved thousands of children gathering in open fields to pray and sing, often in defiance of local authorities.</note> is pleasing to neither God nor man—then one must not deal with the children so cautiously and with such friendly persuasion, as he nevertheless advises; rather, one must earnestly forbid it as a work that is unpleasant to God and men. (Psalm 8:2)</note> and in the prophet **Malachi** in chapter 4, that in the final time, when the great and terrible day of the Lord approaches and the **Antichrist** shall be destroyed therein, He would awaken them to prayer. We have dealt with silent prayer in the chamber above. One should do the one, and not leave the other undone. But he who does not pray out of the Spirit of God in the church will also be unable to pray out of the Spirit of God in the chamber and in secret. Such an uncommon love for prayer / that children give up sleep and food for it / and some even stay fasting until the evening / in order to be all the more fit for prayer: such great patience in frost and cold / and always an unchanging steadfastness in the most difficult weather: the good modesty and order maintained by most in their **prayer hours** <term>Bet-Stunden</term> <note>These were spontaneous or organized gatherings where children met, often in open fields, to pray and sing hymns without adult prompting.</note>; the obedience willingly rendered to their rather sharply-ruling comrades; the special devotion shown by many / the likes of which one is seldom accustomed to find even among old people; the account given by some when required to explain themselves / so well-articulated / that one must marvel at their understanding and their answer: the **deputations** <term>Deputationes</term> / and delegations / which they have sent to authorities and preachers / to obtain power and help: and finally, the [zeal] that broke out among all these many times...

...could be mentioned / if it were not known to us all / how the Devil rages / whenever he sees something truly good rising against his kingdom. **Christ** is therefore set as a sign / which is spoken against. **Luke 2:34.** Against the good **Stephen**, several from the synagogue arose / which is called that of the Freedmen, and of the Cyrenians, and of the Alexandrians, and of those / who were from Cilicia and Asia / but they were not able to resist the wisdom / and the Spirit / who spoke. **Acts 6:9-10.** Just such a gruesome contradiction / and uprising against the movement of the praying children has been shown by the Superintendent of Sorau <note>In the early 1700s, a spontaneous "Children's Prayer" movement (Kinderbeten) broke out in Silesia. I will first take up the circular letter / which reads as follows: **The LORD God is sun and shield!**

->Right Reverend / Much-Honored Gentlemen,<-

It is known not only among us / but also in distant lands / in what manner the children, almost through all of Silesia / have begun to hold unusual prayer hours. However, one would not have suspected / that such a business should also creep in among us / where, praise **God**! no **oppressed church** <note>original: "Ecclesia pressa"; a Latin term for a church suffering under persecution or legal restriction. Continuing on, he proves it sufficiently with this writing of his / that the poor children / to whom he denies and disrupts the extraordinary prayer / in fact have an <term>oppressed church</term> <note>Latin: *Ecclesiam pressam*</note>, or a burdened church / wherein their Christian freedom is greatly aggrieved / and the Spirit is powerfully suppressed. It seems to me exactly as if a heavenly justification had arisen in the hearts of the clergy at Sorau / when they thought to themselves / the Holy Spirit would surely not insult the Holy Ministry so much / as if they were not presiding over their Holy Office sufficiently enough / that he should have had to stir up the children for that reason / who were meant to come to their aid with their prayers / and indeed in that place / where there is a <term>most flourishing church</term> <note>Latin: *Ecclesia Florentissima*</note>, where the Word of GOD is taught purely / and clearly / and the Holy Sacraments are distributed according to Christ’s institution. With the praying children in Silesia <note>A historical region in Central Europe, now mostly in Poland, where the "Children’s Prayer" movement began around 1707 during the Great Northern War.</note> it was of an entirely different nature / as they had no regular churches / there the Holy Spirit might well have done something special / and been able to move <gloss>move</gloss> the children in an unusually special way and manner; however, that would not do in Sorau / where / praise GOD! the public worship was conducted unhindered / and the churches flourished according to the old Saxon / and Lusatian <gloss>Lusatian</gloss> <note>Lusatia is a region in eastern Germany and western Poland where Sorau (modern Żary) is located.</note> manner / and did not require such miraculous children’s prayer. That the *Pietism* <note>A movement within Lutheranism that emphasized personal holiness and emotional devotion over rigid dogma.</note> he has spoken so poorly of / cannot be so evil / since *Christ* himself praised the shouting / and rejoicing of such children. For even if we admit / that the children in *Jerusalem* were moved by a greater degree of the Spirit of GOD / one must still say / that it did not happen without the stirring of the Holy Spirit / that the children in *Sorau* likewise / though in a lesser measure / were moved to a devotion / of which they heard / that their playmates in *Silesia* <note>The "Children’s Prayer" movement began in Silesia in 1707, where children gathered in open fields to pray for peace during the Great Northern War.</note> pray so heartily / from whose company they did not wish to be excluded / but rather to add their own prayer and praise. Whoever has trained senses / can soon notice / that such an impulse / which is contrary to the children's old nature / cannot have the unclean spirit as its author / who is certainly no spirit of prayer / but must stem from him / who is called a Spirit of grace and prayer. Yet this *Inspector* <note>Referring to Erdmann Neumeister, the church superintendent.</note> attributes it to *Belial* <note>A biblical term for the devil or a personification of worthlessness and evil.</note> / who is pregnant with something evil / and [claims] that the unusual prayer of the *Sorau* children was ignited to introduce *Pietism* / in a seemingly secret way. *Belial* could thereby unnoticed lay his *basilisk eggs* <note>In folklore, the basilisk's egg hatched a lethal monster; here it refers to the birth of dangerous ideas.</note> / and hatch *fanaticism, Quakerism,* <note>The Quakers were often used as a "boogeyman" by Lutheran orthodox clergy to describe any group claiming direct inspiration from the Holy Spirit.</note> and similar snakes / and broods of vipers. GOD comfort us / when things have come so far / that one fears regarding the unusual prayer that has arisen / that it were a seed / for *fanaticism,* and *Quakerism,*

to be planted thereby. Which weak-believer would not become shy toward prayer because of this / lest he unintentionally become a *Quaker* / and *enthusiast*. For they do not even know / what *fanatical* / or *Quaker-like* things are. I know well / that otherwise one is ready to *impute* <term>impute</term> <note>To formally attribute a fault or a specific theological error to someone.</note> this or that error / through an often false *logical consequence* <note>original: "Consequenz"</note> / to adults / and the educated, whose hair one wishes to pull; But in all my days I have never heard of children / that any of the heresy-makers was so bold / as to accuse them of such things / because they could have drawn such poison neither from books / nor from themselves; But here in the *circular letter* one throws about nothing but *fanaticism* and *Quakerism* / and fears / that from the children's prayer / through the cunning serpent-like nature of *Belial* / such harmful things and heresies might arise unnoticed. Oh, what bitter words come forth from the Pharisaic spirit! and how close are such words to the sin against the H. Spirit / in that they call the clean spirit / which is in the simple children / an unclean spirit. Has not the LORD our GOD cause to scold such insolent spirits? fighting for pure doctrine and a holy life, and as if it were awake and watchful, he wants to stop the supposed mischief of the <term>praying children</term> in time and prevent the same; and because he believes he has already observed the proper diligence in <term>Sorau</term>, he now also wants to make the necessary arrangements among his <term>fellowship of clergy</term> <note>original Latin: "Confraternität"</note> so that every preacher under his <term>supervision</term> <note>original: "Inſpection"</note> should <note>original Latin: "vigilire"</note> **be vigilant** and not permit such disorder to take root in any of his parishes. I would, however, like to know what kind of <term>consequences</term> <note>original: "ſuiten" (from the French 'suites')</note> such prayer would draw after it, for which this man is so very afraid. Or should those who listened to the children—as happened in <term>Silesia</term>—feel that they were not as moved in the churches as they were by the simple prayer of the children? Or do they perhaps fear the church might remain empty, or at least no longer be visited so diligently? It would certainly have been necessary for Mr. <term>Superintendent</term> to have named such <term>consequences</term>, so that others could have known of them, and the preachers might have had occasion to better understand this matter in its feared evil circumstances and results. But now he will not speak out...

...to name <note>The catchword from the previous page was "nen," completing the word "benennen" or "to name."</note> such **consequences** <note>original: "Suiten"</note> / and meanwhile prescribes some **measures** <note>original: "Messure"</note> to them / as to how they should begin to disrupt the children / and the new method of their prayer. It will not be harmful / he writes / that one should instruct the congregation with **theological leniency** <term>Glimpf</term> <note>A historical term for moderation, propriety, or mildness, often used here sarcastically.</note> and gentleness / to keep the children diligently at the church / at the **Catechism** <note>A summary of Christian principles used for religious instruction.</note> lessons / at the school / and to let them practice prayer / and godliness there. But what kind of theological leniency and gentleness is that to me / that he describes the unusual praying of the children / and their conduct so horribly / as if they were actual fires / that had been set in the city of **Sorau** <note>A city in Lower Lusatia, modern-day Żary, Poland.</note> / as if **Belial** <note>A biblical name for the devil or a personification of ultimate wickedness.</note> went about pregnant under such an **appearance of piety** <note>original Latin: "Specie pietatis"</note> / and wanted to introduce **fanaticism** and **Quakerism** <note>In this era, "Quaker" was often used as a derogatory catch-all term for any radical or "disorderly" spiritual movement.</note> with it / as if there were **basilisk eggs** / yes, snakes / and a brood of vipers hidden beneath / which one wanted to lay down unnoticed. Let that be a "theological leniency and gentleness" for me! Is that his gentleness / Mr. **Neumeister** <note>Erdmann Neumeister (1671–1756), a Lutheran theologian and famous hymn writer who fiercely opposed the emotional Pietist movement.</note> / what then will his wrath be? Is that his leniency / what will it be / when his zeal catches fire? Furthermore, if such things / which he attributed to the prayer of the Sorau children / are truly hidden beneath / if Belial is tucked under there / if basilisk eggs are being hatched / if snakes and a brood of vipers are present / which introduce fanaticism / and Quakerism unnoticed / then one does not need to deal with it so leniently / one must—

<column-break/>

—use a seriousness against it / and put away such harmful things with force. And so that they do not / as it usually tends to happen / make it too clumsy / or too crude / whereby the **intention** <note>original: "Intention"</note> of disrupting the children’s prayer would not be achieved / so the theological leniency and gentleness / of which they in truth know nothing right / nor have ever experienced / shall accomplish it / and promote their purpose. Are these not little pieces of hypocrisy / and pretense / and could a **Machiavelli** <note>Niccolò Machiavelli (1469–1527), the political theorist whose name became a synonym for cunning and cynical statecraft.</note> start a thing more cleverly / when he wants to destroy a matter / that he does not like to see? > O God, you see how poorly things stand in the Church / and that you can begin nothing / no matter how well you mean it / which the lazy workers do not slander. But the inner temple of God / and the altar / and those who worship therein / you shall measure / as it is your church built of living stones / which shall remain for eternity and which the gates of hell shall not overcome. <meta>catchword: already</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>237</page-num>
<header>⸪⸭⸪ (0) ⸫⸭⸫</header>

already were, having arrived there before the 1,000 years, Revelation 19:20; Revelation 20:10. One notices from this clear presentation that there are two entirely distinct great judgments. The first occurs before the <term>Apocalyptic thousand years</term>, and the other proceeds after the completed thousand years. In this latter judgment, nothing more is heard of any Antichrist, nor false prophet, nor the <term>Whore of Babylon</term> <note>A symbolic figure in Revelation representing worldly corruption and false religion</note>, nor trumpets, bowls of wrath, nor the seven thunders. All of these precede the <term>Apocalyptic thousand years</term>, and before these years, the Beast and the false prophet receive their final sentence and are judged. That this is so, however, we can perceive from the contrast between the sixth and the seventh seals. For the entire seventh seal lasts only <term>half a heavenly hour</term>, Revelation 8:1, and follows the sixth seal. If, then, the great, indescribable eternity cannot notoriously be described by such a <term>half a heavenly hour</term>—which would indeed be far too small an expression for it—and thus this <term>silence of half a heavenly hour</term> must still happen within time, then the preceding sixth seal must also still happen within this time. In this time, Christ—the gentle Lamb who bears the sin of the whole world and who, at His first advent in the flesh, did not come to judge the world but to save it—only becomes the Lion in and during His second advent, and judges the world as the Judge of the

<meta>catchword: living</meta>

<summary>The author distinguishes between two distinct judgments: one occurring before the Millennium to destroy the Antichristian powers, and a final judgment following the thousand-year reign. He uses the chronology of the Seals in Revelation to argue that these events occur within a specific temporal framework rather than an immediate transition to eternity.</summary>

If we did not know of the power of children,
the many who now set themselves with might against the children
and their praying could prove it to us well enough
through their opposition; for they are now, without their knowledge
or will, the actual explainers of these words.

<page-num>242</page-num>

...they must [be the enemies], because it is they who are to be overwhelmed and destroyed by the power of **God** through the children and infants. If it were such "childishness," as they say, well then, such armed people would certainly not need to fear them so much; but now that they arm themselves in crowds against this little flock of praying children, they thereby admit that it is no childishness, but that the power of the children is great, against whom they rise up with a collective hand and cry out against from all pulpits. When a place is unknown to us, and we hear of it in the newspapers that a large, considerable army has moved against it, we rightly conclude that the place must not be a small village or a tiny town—for the capture of which such a great mass of people would not be required—but that it must be an important fortress, against which so many troops had to be procured and assembled. Thus, the little children and infants truly have <term>strength</term> <note>original: עז (*oz*)</note> with them, which is not small, because He who is in them is greater than he who is in the world, whom the adversaries serve and whose works they do, in which they must ultimately come up short. These are the enemies for whose sake this power is prepared, of which the Psalmist expressly says: <term>because of your adversaries</term> <note>original: למען צורריך (*l'ma'an tzorerecha*) and ἕνεκα τῶν ἐχθρῶν σου (*heneka tōn echthrōn sou*)</note>, <note>original: in numero plurali</note> in the plural number, in order to show that it is not just one, but many enemies, who can nonetheless be divided specifically into the following heads. The third enemy is the **Babylonian Whore**, which is the great city that holds dominion over the kings of the earth and is set in opposition to the upper city, **Jerusalem**, which comes down from heaven. She has indeed adorned herself with pearls, jewels, gold, and precious stones, but from her secret dealings with the hideous beast upon which she rides, it can already be recognized that she is not such a pure church and virgin as she claims to be; rather, she is full and drunk from the blood of the true virgins of the **Lamb**, and she incites her husband—the ten-horned beast—to the same, which was seen by **John** as being scarlet-colored for that very reason. The fourth enemy is the **false prophet**, or the two-horned beast (Revelation 13:11, compared with Revelation 19:20), which indeed looks like the **Lamb** in whose wool it clothes itself—or, as **Christ** says, it goes about in sheep's clothing—but inwardly it is a ravening wolf; and meanwhile it speaks like the dragon and draws up all evil regulations, <term>edicts</term> <note>original: Edicta</note>, manifestos, proclamations, and bloodthirsty <term>decrees</term> <note>original: Decreta</note>, and executes them through the beast. Furthermore, no child has ever (which would otherwise be ridiculous) set themselves up as a king or prince—for which reason I do not know why anyone should have felt the need to cite the proverb from the **Book of Ecclesiastes** in Chapter 10: *Woe to the land whose king is a child.* <note>Ecclesiastes 10:16. The author is arguing that children leading prayer is not the same as children holding political or ecclesiastical office.</note> Truly, the unity of the Spirit is not abolished by the prayers of such children, but rather promoted. Whether every father and mother of a household—by virtue of their **spiritual priesthood** <note>The Lutheran and Pietist doctrine that all believers have a direct relationship with God and can perform spiritual duties at home.</note>—establishes a small congregation in their house through prayer, or whether the children (who indeed belong to the Church) hold an assembly inside or outside the house, there is no great difference. Just as adult fathers and mothers do not separate themselves from the body of the true Church through their private prayer hours, neither do the children separate themselves from it through their prayer assemblies. On the contrary, as I mentioned above and have heard from true reports in **Silesia** <note>A region then in Prussia/Austria, modern-day Poland, where a massive spontaneous children’s prayer movement occurred in 1707–1708.</note>, and have partly seen myself, the children have gone to church and school much more diligently than before they began such extraordinary prayer exercises.

Ah! Where there is nothing to fear, people are afraid; but where we should hallow the Lord and let Him be our fear and dread, there all fear has vanished. Furthermore, I note from the sermon of the **Superintendent of Sorau** <note>A high-ranking Lutheran official in Sorau (modern Żary, Poland).</note> that he does not want it to seem as if he believed that children "by their years" <note>Literal, physical children.</note> were not meant in the **eighth Psalm**,

<meta>catchword: were/</meta>

<column-break/>

yet when I compare this against the promise he made in a previous sermon—where he said he intended to refute the prayer of the children in Sorau in a specific sermon on the first day of **Pentecost**, and prove from the 8th Psalm that entirely different "children" were understood there—then one sees how he contradicts himself. In that sermon, he considers it indisputable that the text of the 8th Psalm deals with the "chosen property" of Christ, the Apostolic builders, and the companions of His kingdom on earth—namely, all faithful Christians. Thus, one perceives how he is not at one with himself and how his arguments do not hold together. One could actually hear in the sermon how he twisted and turned, and wherever possible, mixed in the "mystical children" <note>A metaphorical interpretation where "children" refers to humble adult believers rather than actual youths.</note> to confound the poor listeners and make them believe he had thereby refuted the children's prayer. Neither **Luther** nor **Selneccer** <note>Nikolaus Selnecker (1530–1592), a prominent Lutheran theologian and hymn writer.</note> (with whom they pride themselves so much in the pulpit) ever set themselves against the prayer of children by their years; rather, they only applied it in the <term>anagogical sense</term> <note>original: "Sensu anagogico." The anagogical sense is a method of interpretation that finds a higher, spiritual, or future meaning in a text beyond the literal words.</note> to the "little ones" and the "unlearned" who possess no worldly wisdom before the world. Such "wise ones according to the flesh" are not the Silesian or Sorau children; otherwise, they would have had many second thoughts about starting such prayer hours. But they are wise in **GOD**, in whom they have believed, and they have performed the prayer to which the Holy Spirit drove them in their childlike hearts without any fear of man.

§. 51. Whatever else was cited in the second and third parts of the sermon—concerning what specific deeds they are, and how great the deeds are through which God prepares a power and praise for Himself out of the mouths of children and through the little and unlearned ones—I shall pass over, because I find nothing therein that might harm my position. However, I cannot leave this unmentioned: the words of Daniel from Chapter 11, where it says in verse 41, *these shall escape out of his hand* <note>Referring to the "hand" of the Antichristian Antiochus. In the Book of Daniel, Antiochus IV Epiphanes is often interpreted by theologians as a "type" or precursor to the final Antichrist.</note>, *even Edom, and Moab, and the chief of the children of Ammon*, have an entirely different meaning than what was suggested. The martyrs are not meant by Edom, nor are those who died at the end trusting in the merit of Christ meant by Moab, nor are the baptized children who died in innocence meant by the "firstfruits of the children of Ammon." Even though **Luther** <note>Martin Luther, the father of the Reformation.</note> was otherwise a man of God, he did not possess such insight into prophetic things to come; thus, this is not a "golden explanation," even if the Lord Superintendent cites it so highly. Throughout the Holy Scriptures, the word Edom, according to the spirit, signifies nothing good. Rather, it refers to all those who set themselves against the Kingdom of Christ, just as the Edomites did against David. According to the prophecies of **Isaiah** in chapters 34 and 63, these shall be destroyed at the end of time when the downfall of the Antichrist occurs. Furthermore, the explanation that Mr. **Neumeister** <note>Erdmann Neumeister (1671–1756), a prominent Lutheran theologian and hymn writer in Sorau who was a fierce opponent of Pietism and the children's prayer movement.</note> provides regarding the "fire of the two witnesses" that 

<column-break/>

consumes their adversaries is also not according to the truth. He believes that the fire signifies the refutation of errors through the Divine Word. Yet, from the example of **Elijah** <note>Reference to 2 Kings 1, where Elijah calls down fire from heaven to consume the soldiers sent to arrest him.</note>—in whose spirit and power the two apocalyptic witnesses shall walk—it is clear and revealed that the fire which then came out of his mouth physically killed and consumed the soldiers sent against him. Also, when Christ appears in **Revelation 19** with the sword of His mouth, He does not engage in long disputations with them or merely refute the errors of the Antichristians; rather, it is the Day of Vengeance, and He **executes** <note>original: "exsequiret"</note> His enemies. That is the consuming power of vengeance which proceeds from His mouth and has no need for a blacksmith’s sword to be held in His hand. His called, chosen, and faithful followers—who follow Christ on white horses during such an execution of the Great Day of Judgment—likewise have no need for such weapons. Nevertheless, an effect <note>original: "effect"</note> like that which the angel had upon the **Assyrian army** <note>A reference to 2 Kings 19:35, where an angel kills 185,000 Assyrian soldiers in a single night without physical combat.</note> is truly introduced without any external weapons, so that they physically die, just as one is struck by a physical sword so that he must die.

... since it is a true kingdom, praised by so many voices in heaven and <term>specifically</term> <note>original: "in specie"</note> by the twenty-four elders, a kingdom upon which they hope with full confidence alongside me, it is no insult to me if I am called a defender and herald of <term>Chiliasm</term> <note>The belief in a literal 1,000-year earthly reign of Christ (the Millennium) before the final judgment.</note>, or of the thousand-year reign of Christ, or even a patriarch of the <term>Chiliasts</term>. be instructed and convinced. No one among his listeners has yet heard me preach, and thus no one has been seduced or led astray by me, as he supposes; therefore, he ought not to have taken it upon his conscience to interfere with what happened in Silesia. The LORD, whose kingdom I believe in and whose honor and truth I love, has not let my work in Silesia be unfruitful; this fruit he shall not take from me, however much he may rage against it. The fly may indeed soil the wall, but it leaves the wall standing, even if it sticks out its sting against it. There shall yet be one flock and one shepherd; the dawn of this began in the time of Christ, but in the end, it will happen even more so, when the fullness of the Gentiles enters in and all Israel is saved. If a group of young children gathers now and prays, what a great multitude will there be in the great assembly when the peoples come together and the kingdoms serve the LORD! **Psalm 102:22.** <note>Petersen cites v. 23; in modern English Bibles this is verse 22: "When the peoples are gathered together, and the kingdoms, to serve the Lord."</note> When the princes among the peoples are gathered as one for the God of Abraham, for God is highly exalted among the shields of the earth. **Psalm 47:9.** <note>Petersen cites v. 10; in English Bibles this is v. 9.</note> When all peoples call upon the name of the LORD and serve him with one accord, **Zephaniah 3:9**, when there shall no longer be so many divisions and sects in the spiritual Israel, nor one tribe set against another,

All these passages bring to light such things that are **not yet fulfilled**, and therefore, if the Scripture is not to be broken, must yet be fulfilled. **§. 55.** Ascend then a high mountain, O **Mother Jerusalem**, and lift up your eyes and look around: all these gathered come to you. # Appendix.

After this my treatise on the Silesian Children <term>Silesian Children: A 1707–1708 movement in Silesia where thousands of children gathered in fields to pray and sing hymns, which Petersen and other Pietists saw as a miraculous sign of the End Times.</term> had already been given over to printing, but because of some hindrances during the past Easter Fair <note>original: "Oster-Messe" — the major book trade fair in Leipzig where new academic and religious works were released.</note> could not be put into production by the publisher, in the meantime Mr. **Erdmann Neumeister’s Counter-Speech** <note>Erdmann Neumeister was a staunch Orthodox Lutheran theologian and famous hymn writer who frequently attacked Petersen’s mystical and chiliastic (millennialist) beliefs.</note> has appeared, which contains nothing other than pure slanders, and uselessly repeats the old stuff of the previous opponents who have long since been refuted by me. Such mockery, however, does no harm at all to the justice of my cause, but will instead be condemned and rejected by posterity as an evil brood; for which reason I do not consider it necessary to answer a single word to it, considering that every impartial person knows that a child of God has something better to do

§. 2. Therefore, I will only briefly extract one or two points and shortly answer what I deem necessary. He writes that I have borne false witness by saying that the Lord Christ has not yet taken up His great power, but rather wrote that such a thing would only happen at the seventh trumpet. Let him settle that with the one who, through His Spirit, testified to such things in the Holy Scripture—from which I have learned through that same Spirit. For in Revelation 11:15, 16, 17, it says:

> The kingdoms of the world are become the kingdoms of our Lord, and of his Christ; and he shall reign for ever and ever. And the four and twenty elders, which sat before God on their seats, fell upon their faces, and worshipped God, and said: We give thee thanks, O Lord God Almighty,

For although He already rules now, even in the midst of His enemies, and although both this world and the world to come are subject to Him (Ephesians 1:21), we do not yet see, <term>we do not yet see</term> <note>original Greek: οὔπω ὁρῶμεν (*oupo horomen*)</note>, that everything is subject to Him (Hebrews 2:8). This is because He has not yet taken up His great power or revealed Himself in it, of which the Prophet Isaiah also testifies in the second chapter: that <term>in that day</term> <note>original Hebrew: ביום ההוא (*ba-yom ha-hu*)</note> the Lord alone shall be exalted. Even though He is already exalted now, He is not yet seen in such glory, nor seen <term>with such emphasis</term> <note>original Latin: *cum emphasi* — meaning in a fully realized or ultimate sense.</note>; and He will be seen even more when, in the successive ages to come, He further subjects all enemies to Himself, until it is finally said that He, with the Father and the Holy Spirit, shall be God, all in all (1 Corinthians 15). Let him [Neumeister] then dispute this with the Holy Spirit.

Scribes <note>Original: *Schriftgelehrten*. §. 4. Thus he also does me an injustice when he writes that I had mocked him because his father was a schoolmaster. I did not know this at all until now, when I read it from his own hand. It matters nothing to me what lineage a person has according to external appearances, because I hold a pious sexton and schoolmaster far higher than a highly learned, yet quarrelsome and pompous Superintendent <note>A high-ranking administrative official in the Lutheran church.</note> who lacks true piety.
